Visual witness description of PDF page 4 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A path diagram for "segment a" showing four relations between boundary label sets. On the left boundary, there are label sets $D^+$ (with label "entry" to its left) and $D^-$ (with label "exit" to its left), connected by a downward curved arrow labeled $L_a$ from $D^+$ to $D^-$. On the right boundary, there are label sets $D^+$ (with label "exit" to its right) and $D^-$ (with label "entry" to its right), connected by an upward curved arrow labeled $R_a$ from $D^-$ to $D^+$. A horizontal arrow pointing from left $D^+$ to right $D^+$ is labeled $F_a$. A horizontal arrow pointing from right $D^-$ to left $D^-$ is labeled $B_a$. Centered between the arrows is the text "segment a".
```


Place diagrams in a line. At each internal boundary, identify an exit with the entry of the neighboring diagram bearing the same label. The product records finite paths from an outer entry to the first outer exit, with all earlier joins internal. A traversal of any consecutive block can be replaced by one edge of the block’s product, and that edge can conversely be expanded into a finite traversal. This proves associativity. The diagram with $F$ and $B$ identity relations and $L,R$ empty is the identity. Thus the diagrams form a finite monoid $\mathcal T_m$.

This is the degree-$m$ monoid of partitioned binary relations of Martin and Mazorchuk (Martin and Mazorchuk 2013, secs. 2.1–2.3) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[MartinMazorchuk2013](#ref-MartinMazorchuk2013)]. To identify the two descriptions, label $D^+$ and $D^-$ by a common $m$-element set. At each boundary, an entry and an exit with the same underlying label give one vertex. The four relations above become the four blocks of a binary relation on the left and right vertex sets. A join always switches from one factor to its neighbor, exactly as in their alternating-path composition. We have included the path expansion and contraction proof of associativity to fix the finite-path convention used below.

<a id="diag:product"></a>
Write $a\sqsubseteq b$ when all four relations of $a$ are included in the corresponding relations of $b$. Products preserve this order in each argument. Joining two diagrams at one boundary gives $$\label{diag:product}
\begin{aligned}
 F_{ab}&=F_a(L_bR_a)^*F_b,&
 B_{ab}&=B_b(R_aL_b)^*B_a,\\
 L_{ab}&=L_a\cup F_a(L_bR_a)^*L_bB_a,&
 R_{ab}&=R_b\cup B_bR_a(L_bR_a)^*F_b.
\end{aligned}$$ For example, a forward path crosses $a$, alternates returns at the join, and crosses $b$. Reflection exchanges $D^+$ with $D^-$, $F$ with $B$, and $L$ with $R$, while reversing product order. We will prove symmetric statements for the positive sign and obtain the negative sign by this reflection.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### From complementation to an order-reversing image

The algebraic obstruction to complementation is the following bound. As in the introduction, $\mathcal R_H$ is the monoid of all binary relations on $H$, composed in path order.

<a id="stmt-amp-relation-bound"></a>
<div id="amp:relation-bound" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Order-reversing image bound). *<span id="amp:image" label="amp:image"></span> Let $m\geq1$, let $H$ be a finite set of cardinality $h\geq2$, and let $S_0$ be a submonoid of $\mathcal T_m$. Suppose that a surjective multiplicative map $\phi_0:S_0\to\mathcal R_H$ satisfies <a id="amp:reverse"></a>$$z\sqsubseteq w\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \phi_0(w)\subseteq\phi_0(z)
 \qquad(z,w\in S_0).
 \label{amp:reverse}$$ Then <a id="amp:bound"></a>$$2m\geq 2^{\lfloor(h-2)/127\rfloor}.
 \label{amp:bound}$$ The map need not initially be assumed to preserve identities.*

</div>

The proof is completed in Section <a href="#amp:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:section">4</a>. We first explain the automata reduction, using the finite-computation representation below. Its construction, proved in Section <a href="#auto:finite-computations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:finite-computations">5.2</a>, uses one additional state to represent success by an exit past the right endmarker. Local diagram edges record finitely many stay moves followed by one move out of a cell; no bound on repeated crossings is imposed.

<a id="stmt-auto-representation"></a>
<div id="auto:representation" class="lemma">

<a id="auto:recognition"></a>
<a id="auto:context-monotonicity"></a>
**Lemma 3**. *Let $B$ be an $s$-state 2NFA over a finite alphabet $\Sigma$ in the model fixed in the introduction. There are a monoid homomorphism $$\tau:\Sigma^*\longrightarrow\mathcal T_{s+1},$$ two fixed endmarker diagrams $\lambda,\rho\in\mathcal T_{s+1}$, and fixed positive labels $a,b$ such that, for every word $w$, $$\label{auto:recognition}
  w\in L(B)
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  (a,b)\in F_{\lambda\tau(w)\rho}.$$ Consequently, if $\tau(u)\sqsubseteq\tau(v)$, then $$\label{auto:context-monotonicity}
  \alpha u\beta\in L(B)
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \alpha v\beta\in L(B)
  \qquad(\alpha,\beta\in\Sigma^*).$$*

</div>

The acceptance equivalence immediately implies the stated monotonicity in arbitrary word contexts:

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-representation"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-representation</summary>

*Derivation of context monotonicity.* Diagram multiplication is increasing in each factor. If $\tau(u)\sqsubseteq\tau(v)$, then $$\lambda\tau(\alpha)\tau(u)\tau(\beta)\rho
 \ \sqsubseteq\
 \lambda\tau(\alpha)\tau(v)\tau(\beta)\rho.$$ The designated forward edge persists under this inclusion. Applying <a href="#auto:recognition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="auto:recognition">[auto:recognition]</a> proves <a href="#auto:context-monotonicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="auto:context-monotonicity">[auto:context-monotonicity]</a>. ◻

</details>

For relation-product emptiness, singleton contexts convert this increasing behavior of acceptance into a decreasing relation image. For $F\subseteq H$, write $I_F=\{(x,x):x\in F\}$. A multiplicative map is *unital* if it preserves identities.

<a id="stmt-auto-order-reversal"></a>
<div id="auto:order-reversal" class="proposition">

<a id="auto:reverse-order"></a>
**Proposition 4**. *Let $H$ be a finite set with at least two elements. If an $s$-state 2NFA recognizes $\Sigma_H^*\setminus L_H$, then a submonoid of $\mathcal T_{s+1}$ admits a surjective unital homomorphism $\phi$ onto $\mathcal R_H$ satisfying $$\label{auto:reverse-order}
  z\sqsubseteq z'
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \phi(z')\subseteq\phi(z).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-order-reversal"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-order-reversal</summary>

<a id="auto:word-order"></a>
*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#auto:representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:representation">3</a> to the complementing machine, and let $S=\tau(\Sigma_H^*)$. Suppose $\tau(u)\sqsubseteq\tau(v)$. For each $p,q\in H$, take the one-letter words $$\alpha=I_{\{p\}},\qquad \beta=I_{\{q\}}.$$ They belong to $\Sigma_H$, and $$I_{\{p\}}r(u)I_{\{q\}}
 =
 \begin{cases}
   \{(p,q)\},& (p,q)\in r(u),\\
   \varnothing,&(p,q)\notin r(u).
 \end{cases}$$ Since the machine accepts exactly the words with empty relation product, <a href="#auto:context-monotonicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="auto:context-monotonicity">[auto:context-monotonicity]</a> implies $$(p,q)\notin r(u)
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  (p,q)\notin r(v).$$ Thus $$\label{auto:word-order}
  \tau(u)\sqsubseteq\tau(v)
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  r(v)\subseteq r(u).$$

If $\tau(u)=\tau(v)$, applying <a href="#auto:word-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="auto:word-order">[auto:word-order]</a> in both directions yields $r(u)=r(v)$. Therefore $$\phi:S\longrightarrow\mathcal R_H,
  \qquad \phi(\tau(w))=r(w),$$ is well-defined. Concatenation of words proves multiplicativity, and the empty word proves preservation of the identity. It is surjective because every relation in $\mathcal R_H$ is itself a letter of $\Sigma_H$. Equation <a href="#auto:word-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="auto:word-order">[auto:word-order]</a> is exactly the required order reversal <a href="#auto:reverse-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="auto:reverse-order">[auto:reverse-order]</a>. ◻

</details>

With $m=s+1$, Theorem <a href="#amp:image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:image">[amp:image]</a> therefore bounds the states of a complementing machine. Section <a href="#auto:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:section">5</a> constructs the $h+2$-state source machine and completes the numerical deduction. It remains to prove the algebraic bound. The next subsection associates at most $2m$ recurrent classes with an idempotent diagram. We will measure loss by the classes whose through loops are missing, and force that loss to grow exponentially with $h$.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Recurrent classes in a corner

<a id="diag:idempotent"></a>
For an idempotent $e\in\mathcal T_m$, define <a id="diag:KP"></a>$$\label{diag:KP}
\begin{aligned}
 K_e^+&=(L_eR_e)^*,& P_e^+&=K_e^+F_eK_e^+,\\
 K_e^-&=(R_eL_e)^*,& P_e^-&=K_e^-B_eK_e^-.
\end{aligned}$$ When working only with the positive sign we omit superscripts. By $e^2=e$ and <a href="#diag:product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="diag:product">[diag:product]</a>, $$\label{diag:idempotent}
 F_eK_eF_e=F_e,\qquad P_e^2=P_e.$$ In particular, $P_e$ is transitive. A point $x\in D^\sigma$ is *recurrent* if $xP_e^\sigma x$. Mutual relatedness under $P_e^\sigma$ is an equivalence relation on the recurrent points. Let $\mathcal C_e$ be the collection of its classes for both signs, keeping the signs distinct. Then <a id="diag:class-count"></a>$$\label{diag:class-count}
 |\mathcal C_e|\le2m.$$ For the identity diagram, $K_e^\sigma=P_e^\sigma$ is the identity relation on $D^\sigma$, so all $2m$ directional labels form singleton recurrent classes.

<a id="diag:corner"></a>
<a id="diag:corner-absorb"></a>
The *corner* $e\mathcal T_me=\{eae:a\in\mathcal T_m\}$ is a monoid with identity $e$; membership is equivalent to $ez=ze=z$. For $z$ in this corner put $$U_e^+(z)=K_e^+F_zK_e^+,\qquad
 U_e^-(z)=K_e^-B_zK_e^-.$$ The return formulas and the two corner identities give $$\label{diag:corner}
 L_e\subseteq L_z,\quad R_e\subseteq R_z,\qquad
 F_z=F_e(L_zR_e)^*F_z=F_z(L_eR_z)^*F_e.$$ Consequently $$\label{diag:corner-absorb}
 F_eK_eF_z\subseteq F_z,\quad F_zK_eF_e\subseteq F_z,
 \qquad P_eU_e(z),\ U_e(z)P_e\subseteq U_e(z).$$ The last two inclusions follow by multiplying the first two by $K_e$ on the appropriate sides. If $x,y$ belong to one recurrent class, then $yP_ex$, $xP_ey$, and <a href="#diag:corner-absorb" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="diag:corner-absorb">[diag:corner-absorb]</a> show that a loop $xU_e(z)x$ implies $yU_e(z)y$. Reflection gives the same fact for the negative sign. Hence the following is well-defined: $$\mathcal M_e(z)=\{C\in\mathcal C_e:
       xU_e^\sigma(z)x\text{ fails for }x\in C,
       \text{ where }C\subseteq D^\sigma\}.$$ We call $\mathcal M_e(z)$ the *missing set* of $z$ relative to $e$.

<a id="stmt-diag-missing"></a>
<div id="diag:missing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Products of missing sets). *Let $e\in\mathcal T_m$ be idempotent. For $z,w\in e\mathcal T_me$, $$\mathcal M_e(zw)\subseteq\mathcal M_e(z)\cup\mathcal M_e(w),
 \qquad \mathcal M_e(e)=\varnothing.$$ In particular, $\mathcal M_e(z^k)\subseteq\mathcal M_e(z)$ for every positive integer $k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-diag-missing"><summary>Proof of stmt-diag-missing</summary>

*Proof.* The returns of $e$ persist in both $z$ and $w$, so $(L_wR_z)^*\supseteq K_e$. Formula <a href="#diag:product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="diag:product">[diag:product]</a> therefore gives $U_e^+(zw)\supseteq U_e^+(z)U_e^+(w)$. Two loops at the same point compose to a loop. For the negative sign the corresponding product is $U_e^-(w)U_e^-(z)$, which gives the same conclusion. Finally $U_e^\sigma(e)=P_e^\sigma$, so no recurrent class is missing from $e$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-diag-rectangles"></a>
<div id="diag:rectangles" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Rectangles of through edges). *Let $e\in\mathcal T_m$ be idempotent and let $z\in e\mathcal T_me$. For a positive recurrent class $C\in\mathcal C_e$ and any $p\in C$, set $$A_C=\{u:u(F_eK_e)p\},\qquad
 B_C=\{v:p(K_eF_e)v\}.$$ The rectangle $A_C\times B_C$ is independent of $p$ and is contained in $F_e$. These rectangles cover $F_e$. If $C\notin\mathcal M_e(z)$, its rectangle is contained in $F_z$. The reflected assertions hold for negative classes and $B_e$. In particular, <a id="diag:detect"></a>$$\label{diag:detect}
 \mathcal M_e(z)=\varnothing\quad\Longrightarrow\quad e\sqsubseteq z.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-diag-rectangles"><summary>Proof of stmt-diag-rectangles</summary>

*Proof.* Containment in $F_e$ follows from $F_eK_eF_e=F_e$. The identities $$(F_eK_e)P_e=F_eK_e,\qquad P_e(K_eF_e)=K_eF_e$$ show that replacing $p$ by a mutually $P_e$-related point does not change either factor of the rectangle.

To prove coverage, <a href="#diag:idempotent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="diag:idempotent">[diag:idempotent]</a> implies $F_eP_e^kF_e=F_e$ for every $k\ge1$. Given a pair in $F_e$, choose a witness for this expression with $k>m$. Some label repeats along the $P_e$ portion. That label $p$ is recurrent, since a nonempty $P_e$-path from $p$ back to itself is a $P_e$-edge by transitivity. The portions before and after $p$ belong to $F_eK_e$ and $K_eF_e$: indeed $F_eP_e^j\subseteq F_eK_e$ and $P_e^jF_e\subseteq K_eF_e$ for $j\ge0$, including $j=0$ since $K_e$ is reflexive.

If $C$ is not missing from $z$, insert $pK_eF_zK_ep$ between the two halves of its rectangle. The resulting relation is contained in $F_eK_eF_zK_eF_e\subseteq F_z$ by <a href="#diag:corner-absorb" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="diag:corner-absorb">[diag:corner-absorb]</a>. Reflection proves the backward assertion. When the missing set is empty all through edges of $e$ are thus present in $z$, and its return edges persist by <a href="#diag:corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="diag:corner">[diag:corner]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Transport through a fixed context

The next statement controls a whole family of replacements at once. The common target set, rather than merely a bound for each replacement, will be essential in the amplification argument.

<a id="stmt-diag-transport"></a>
<div id="diag:transport" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Transport). *Let $e,d\in\mathcal T_m$ be idempotents and let $u,v\in\mathcal T_m$ satisfy $uev=d$. For every $J\subseteq\mathcal C_e$ there exists a single set $J'\subseteq\mathcal C_d$, with $|J'|\le2|J|$, such that $$\mathcal M_d(uzv)\subseteq J'$$ for every $z\in e\mathcal T_me$ satisfying $\mathcal M_e(z)\subseteq J$ and $uzv\in d\mathcal T_md$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-diag-transport"><summary>Proof of stmt-diag-transport</summary>

*Proof.* For each positive class of $\mathcal C_d$, choose a representative $x$ and a witness for $xK_d^+F_{uev}K_d^+x$. Expand the middle edge into an actual finite path through the three diagrams $u,e,v$. For a negative class do the same with $xK_d^-B_{uev}K_d^-x$. Make these choices once, independently of $z$.

For every occurrence of a through edge of the middle factor $e$ on a chosen path, choose an $e$-class whose rectangle covers that edge, using Lemma <a href="#diag:rectangles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:rectangles">6</a>. Mark a $d$-class if any class chosen along its witness lies in $J$; let $J'$ be the marked set. If a class is unmarked, all its chosen through edges survive replacement of $e$ by $z$, as do all return edges of $e$. Its original outer $K_d^\sigma$ pieces remain fixed. Thus its witness is a loop in $U_d^\sigma(uzv)$, and the class is not missing. This proves the required common-set inclusion.

It remains to count marked classes. For each $d$-class $C$, let $S_C$ be the set of $e$-classes chosen along its fixed witness. We show that the sets $S_C$ are pairwise disjoint among $d$-classes of a fixed sign. Consider two positive witnesses, written as $$xK_d^+\alpha\;F_d\;\beta K_d^+x,
 \qquad yK_d^+\alpha'\;F_d\;\beta'K_d^+y,$$ where $\alpha,\beta,\alpha',\beta'\in D^+$ and the displayed $F_d$-edges have been expanded through $u,e,v$. Suppose the expanded paths use occurrences of middle-factor edges $(a,b)$ and $(a',b')$ covered by one $e$-class. Its rectangle also contains $(a,b')$ and $(a',b)$. The sign of that $e$-class fixes the orientation of both edges and their entry and exit boundaries inside the three-factor product. Thus the prefix ending at $a$, the crossed edge $(a,b')$, and the suffix starting at $b'$ concatenate to a path from $\alpha$ to $\beta'$. Neither retained subpath has an earlier outer exit. The concatenation is therefore a valid finite path for $F_d$, even if it repeats internal crossings or its selected middle edge points backward. Interchanging the two prefixes and suffixes similarly gives $\alpha'F_d\beta$.

The original exterior factors now give $xP_d^+y$ and $yP_d^+x$, so $x$ and $y$ belong to the same recurrent class. Reflection gives the same conclusion for negative witnesses, with $B_d$ in place of $F_d$. This proves the asserted disjointness for each sign. A witness using only returns of $e$ has $S_C=\varnothing$ and is never marked. Since $J'=\{C:S_C\cap J\ne\varnothing\}$, at most $|J|$ classes of each sign are marked, and $|J'|\le2|J|$. ◻

</details>

<a id="nest:section"></a>
### A loss budget for nested idempotents

For idempotents in a semigroup, write $d\trianglelefteq e$ if $de=ed=d$, and say that $d$ is *nested under* $e$. This is a partial order: reflexivity and antisymmetry are immediate, and if $d\trianglelefteq e\trianglelefteq f$, then $df=(de)f=d(ef)=de=d$ and $fd=f(ed)=(fe)d=ed=d$. Nesting is distinct from the edge-inclusion order $\sqsubseteq$.

We will bound the sum of the losses incurred along a nested chain, even though the identity, the recurrent classes, and hence the meaning of a loss change at every step. The key fact is a containment dichotomy: a new recurrent class contains either an old class that is not lost, or at least two old classes.

<a id="stmt-nest-absorption"></a>
<div id="nest:absorption" class="lemma">

<a id="nest:through-absorption"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Absorption under nesting). *Let $d,e\in\mathcal T_m$ be idempotents with $d\trianglelefteq e$. For either sign $\sigma$, $$K_e^\sigma\subseteq K_d^\sigma,
 \qquad
 P_e^\sigma P_d^\sigma\subseteq P_d^\sigma,
 \qquad
 P_d^\sigma P_e^\sigma\subseteq P_d^\sigma.$$ In the positive sign one also has $$\label{nest:through-absorption}
 F_eP_d^+\subseteq P_d^+,
 \qquad P_d^+F_e\subseteq P_d^+.$$ The corresponding negative-sign inclusions use $B_e$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-absorption"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-absorption</summary>

<a id="nest:forward-identities"></a>
<a id="nest:return-identities"></a>
*Proof.* We prove the positive-sign statements and suppress the superscript $+$. Because $d\in e\mathcal T_m e$, its returns contain those of $e$: $L_e\subseteq L_d$ and $R_e\subseteq R_d$. Set $$S=(L_dR_e)^*,\qquad T=(L_eR_d)^*.$$ Then $K_e\subseteq S,T\subseteq K_d$. The multiplication formulas applied to $ed=de=d$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 F_d&=F_eSF_d=F_dTF_e,\label{nest:forward-identities}\\
 L_d&=L_e\cup F_eSL_dB_e,
 &R_d&=R_e\cup B_eR_dTF_e.
 \label{nest:return-identities}
\end{aligned}$$

We spell out the two star rearrangements needed below, using the finite-path identities in <a href="#diag:star" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="diag:star">[diag:star]</a>. Expanding $R_d$ in $K_d=(L_dR_d)^*$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 K_d
 &=\bigl(L_dR_e\cup L_dB_eR_dTF_e\bigr)^*\\
 &=S\bigl(L_dB_eR_dTF_eS\bigr)^*,\\
 F_eK_dF_d
 &=\bigl(F_eSL_dB_eR_dT\bigr)^*F_eSF_d\\
 &=\bigl(F_eSL_dB_eR_dT\bigr)^*F_d
 \subseteq K_dF_d.
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, $F_eSL_dB_e\subseteq L_d$ by <a href="#nest:return-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:return-identities">[nest:return-identities]</a>, so the relation inside the last star is contained in $L_dR_dT\subseteq K_d$. For the other side, expand $L_d$ instead: $$\begin{aligned}
 K_d
 &=\bigl(L_eR_d\cup F_eSL_dB_eR_d\bigr)^*\\
 &=T\bigl(F_eSL_dB_eR_dT\bigr)^*,\\
 F_dK_dF_e
 &=F_dTF_e\bigl(SL_dB_eR_dTF_e\bigr)^*\\
 &=F_d\bigl(SL_dB_eR_dTF_e\bigr)^*
 \subseteq F_dK_d.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $B_eR_dTF_e\subseteq R_d$, so the relation inside the star is contained in $SL_dR_d\subseteq K_d$. These inclusions, multiplied by the remaining $K_d$, prove <a href="#nest:through-absorption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:through-absorption">[nest:through-absorption]</a> because $P_d=K_dF_dK_d$. Finally, $K_dP_d=P_dK_d=P_d$ and $K_e\subseteq K_d$ imply $$P_eP_d=K_eF_eK_eP_d\subseteq P_d,
 \qquad P_dP_e=P_dK_eF_eK_e\subseteq P_d.$$ Reflection proves all negative-sign statements. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-nest-containment"></a>
<div id="nest:containment" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Containment of recurrent classes). *Let $d,e\in\mathcal T_m$ be idempotents with $d\trianglelefteq e$. Every class in $\mathcal C_d$ contains either*

1.  *a whole class in $\mathcal C_e\setminus\mathcal M_e(d)$; or*

2.  *two distinct whole classes in $\mathcal C_e$.*

*All classes in either containment have the same sign.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-containment"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-containment</summary>

<a id="nest:factored-witness"></a>
*Proof.* Again work in the positive sign and omit its superscript. Fix a recurrent point $x$ of $P_d$. Using <a href="#nest:forward-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:forward-identities">[nest:forward-identities]</a> twice, its recurrence gives $$x\bigl(K_dF_eSF_dTF_eK_d\bigr)x.$$ By the rectangle cover in Lemma <a href="#diag:rectangles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:rectangles">6</a>, each of the two displayed $F_e$-edges can be factored through an $e$-recurrent point. Denote the first point by $p$ and the second by $q$. The resulting witness has the form $$\label{nest:factored-witness}
 x\,\alpha\,p\,\beta\,q\,\gamma\,x,$$ where $$\begin{aligned}
 \alpha&=K_dF_eK_e,\\
 \beta&=K_eF_eSF_dTF_eK_e=K_eF_dK_e=U_e^+(d),\\
 \gamma&=K_eF_eK_d.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\beta\subseteq P_d$ because $K_e\subseteq K_d$. Moreover, Lemma <a href="#nest:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:absorption">8</a> shows that multiplying $P_d$ on either side by $\alpha$ or $\gamma$ gives a subrelation of $P_d$. Thus all four endpoint relations needed for class membership follow from <a href="#nest:factored-witness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:factored-witness">[nest:factored-witness]</a> and $xP_dx$: $$\begin{array}{ll}
 x\,P_d\,x\,\alpha\,p &\Longrightarrow xP_dp,\\
 p\,\beta\,q\,\gamma\,x &\Longrightarrow pP_dx,\\
 x\,\alpha\,p\,\beta\,q &\Longrightarrow xP_dq,\\
 q\,\gamma\,x\,P_d\,x &\Longrightarrow qP_dx.
\end{array}$$ In particular $p$ and $q$ are recurrent for $P_d$ and belong to the class of $x$. In the first and last lines, inserting the recurrence at $x$ is essential: the shorter witness portion alone need not contain a $d$-through edge.

The entire $P_e$-class of $p$ lies in the $P_d$-class of $x$. Indeed, if $r$ is in the former class, then $xP_dpP_er$ and $rP_epP_dx$, and the two mixed absorptions of Lemma <a href="#nest:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:absorption">8</a> give $xP_dr$ and $rP_dx$. The same argument applies to $q$. If their $P_e$-classes are distinct, this proves the second alternative. If they coincide, then $pU_e^+(d)q$ by <a href="#nest:factored-witness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="nest:factored-witness">[nest:factored-witness]</a> and $qP_ep$. The corner absorption $U_e^+(d)P_e\subseteq U_e^+(d)$ gives $pU_e^+(d)p$, so that class is not in $\mathcal M_e(d)$. This proves the first alternative. Reflection handles the negative sign. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-nest-chain"></a>
<div id="nest:chain" class="proposition">

**Proposition 10** (Chain bound). *Let $b_0,b_1,\ldots,b_k\in\mathcal T_m$ be idempotents such that $b_j\trianglelefteq b_{j-1}$ for $1\leq j\leq k$. Suppose a single set $J_0\subseteq\mathcal C_{b_0}$ satisfies $$\mathcal M_{b_0}(b_j)\subseteq J_0
 \qquad(0\leq j\leq k).$$ Then <a id="nest:chain-bound"></a>$$\label{nest:chain-bound}
 \sum_{j=1}^k\bigl|\mathcal M_{b_{j-1}}(b_j)\bigr|
 \leq 2|J_0|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-nest-chain"><summary>Proof of stmt-nest-chain</summary>

*Proof.* Choose one *protected* label in every class of $\mathcal C_{b_0}\setminus J_0$, and keep these choices fixed. Every protected label $x$ of sign $\sigma$ satisfies $xU_{b_0}^\sigma(b_j)x$ at every stage $j$. By transitivity of nesting and Lemma <a href="#nest:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:absorption">8</a>, $K_{b_0}^\sigma\subseteq K_{b_j}^\sigma$, so this loop also belongs to $P_{b_j}^\sigma$. Thus every protected label remains recurrent. Let $s_j$ count the classes in $\mathcal C_{b_j}$ containing no protected label. In particular $s_0=|J_0|$.

Write $\delta_j=|\mathcal M_{b_{j-1}}(b_j)|$. Every class missing at step $j$ is unprotected. Otherwise it would contain a protected label $x$ of sign $\sigma$; the loop $xU_{b_0}^\sigma(b_j)x$ and the inclusion $K_{b_0}^\sigma\subseteq K_{b_{j-1}}^\sigma$ would then give $xU_{b_{j-1}}^\sigma(b_j)x$, contradicting missingness.

Assign weight $1/2$ to each missing unprotected class of $\mathcal C_{b_{j-1}}$, and weight $1$ to each other unprotected class there. Their total weight is $s_{j-1}-\delta_j/2$. Figure <a href="#fig:nesting-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:nesting-budget">2</a> illustrates the two ways that an unprotected new class can obtain weight at least one from the previous stage.

<figure id="fig:nesting-budget">

<figcaption>The weighted containment argument in the chain bound. All classes shown have one fixed sign and contain no protected label. Each new class contains either a whole nonmissing old class or two distinct whole old classes. A missing old class has weight $1/2$; every other old class has weight $1$. The displayed regions express set containment schematically. Distinct new classes cannot use the same whole old class, so their unit weight requirements add.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-nesting-budget"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:nesting-budget`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,thick]
 \draw[rounded corners] (0,0) rectangle (5,2.3);
 \draw[rounded corners] (6,0) rectangle (11,2.3);
 \node at (2.5,1.95) {one new unprotected class};
 \node at (8.5,1.95) {one new unprotected class};
 \node[draw,rounded corners,fill=blue!7,align=center,
       inner xsep=6pt,inner ysep=5pt] at (2.5,0.95)
       {old nonmissing class\\weight $1$};
 \node[draw,dashed,rounded corners,fill=black!4,align=center,
       inner xsep=6pt,inner ysep=5pt] at (7.3,0.95)
       {old class\\weight $\ge\frac12$};
 \node[draw,dashed,rounded corners,fill=black!4,align=center,
       inner xsep=6pt,inner ysep=5pt] at (9.7,0.95)
       {old class\\weight $\ge\frac12$};
 \node at (5.5,1.1) {or};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 10 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Schematic diagram showing two alternative cases separated by the label "or". On the left: a rounded rectangle labeled "one new unprotected class" enclosing an inner solid rectangle labeled "old nonmissing class" and "weight 1". On the right: a rounded rectangle labeled "one new unprotected class" enclosing two adjacent dashed rectangles side by side, each labeled "old class" and "weight $\ge \frac{1}{2}$".
```


By Lemma <a href="#nest:containment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:containment">9</a>, each unprotected class at stage $j$ contains previous classes of total weight at least $1$: either one nonmissing class, or two distinct classes. Every contained previous class is unprotected, since a protected label in it would also protect the new class. Distinct new classes cannot use the same previous class, because classes of a fixed sign are disjoint and the two sign-label sets are disjoint. Consequently $$s_j\leq s_{j-1}-\frac{\delta_j}{2}.$$ Summing and using $s_k\geq0$ yields $\sum_{j=1}^k\delta_j\leq2(s_0-s_k)\leq2|J_0|$. ◻

</details>

<a id="amp:section"></a>
### An exponential bound for order-reversing images

We now prove Theorem <a href="#amp:image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:image">[amp:image]</a>, combining transport and the nested-chain inequality to bound order-reversing relation images. Write $\mathcal R_H$ for the monoid of all binary relations on a finite set $H$, with multiplication in path order. For $F\subseteq H$, write $I_F=\{(x,x):x\in F\}$; relations on $F$ are also regarded as relations on $H$ supported on $F\times F$.

The proof amplifies one off-diagonal pair in the image into a family of pair additions. The chain inequality bounds their total cost in missing recurrent classes; restriction to smaller relation monoids bounds each cost from below. We first record two elementary facts that allow these restrictions without losing the hypotheses. The pattern of relation additions follows the matching-diagram argument in (OpenAI 2026, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[CompanionDeterminization2026](#ref-CompanionDeterminization2026)]; all loss estimates needed here are supplied by the path-diagram results just proved.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Corners and unit lifts

For an idempotent $d$ in a semigroup $A$, its *corner* $dAd=\{dad:a\in A\}$ is a monoid with identity $d$. A *unit* means an element with a two-sided inverse relative to the specified monoid identity.

<a id="stmt-amp-minimal-corner"></a>
<div id="amp:minimal-corner" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Minimal idempotent corners). *Let $A$ be a finite semigroup, let $M$ be a monoid, and let $\theta:A\to M$ be a surjective multiplicative map. For every idempotent $q\in M$, there is an idempotent $d\in A$ with $\theta(d)=q$ that is minimal among such idempotents under $\trianglelefteq$. For every such minimal $d$, restriction of $\theta$ gives a unital surjection $$dAd\longrightarrow qMq.$$ Every element of $dAd$ whose image is a unit of $qMq$ is itself a unit of $dAd$. In particular, every unit of $qMq$ has a unit lift.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-amp-minimal-corner"><summary>Proof of stmt-amp-minimal-corner</summary>

*Proof.* Every element $w$ of a finite semigroup has an idempotent positive power: the sequence of powers is eventually periodic, so a sufficiently large exponent divisible by its eventual period gives $w^{2k}=w^k$. Applying this to a preimage of $q$ gives an idempotent preimage of $q$. Finiteness then gives one minimal in the nesting order.

For any $y\in qMq$, choose $x\in A$ with $\theta(x)=y$. Then $\theta(dxd)=qyq=y$, proving surjectivity on the corner; its identity $d$ maps to $q$. Now let $w\in dAd$ have unit image. An idempotent power $w^k$ has an image that is both a unit and an idempotent in $qMq$, hence equals $q$. Since $w^k\trianglelefteq d$, minimality gives $w^k=d$. If $k>1$, the element $w^{k-1}$ is a two-sided inverse of $w$ in $dAd$; if $k=1$, then $w=d$ is already the identity. ◻

</details>

The next lemma identifies a smaller full relation monoid inside an idempotent corner. Its hypothesis says that the old relation restricts to the identity on the retained points.

<a id="stmt-amp-relation-corner"></a>
<div id="amp:relation-corner" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Restriction of a relation corner). *Let $P\in\mathcal R_H$ be idempotent, let $F\subseteq H$, and put $i=I_F$. Suppose that $iPi=i$, and define $Q=PiP$. Then $Q$ is idempotent, $Q\trianglelefteq P$, and restriction is a unital monoid isomorphism <a id="amp:restriction"></a>$$\rho:Q\mathcal R_HQ\longrightarrow\mathcal R_F,
 \qquad \rho(Z)=iZi.
 \label{amp:restriction}$$ Its inverse sends $A\in\mathcal R_F$ to $PAP$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-amp-relation-corner"><summary>Proof of stmt-amp-relation-corner</summary>

*Proof.* Using $P^2=P$ and $iPi=i$, we obtain $$Q^2=Q,\qquad PQ=QP=Q,\qquad
 Qi=Pi,\qquad iQ=iP,\qquad iQi=i.$$ If $Z=QZQ$, then $PZP=Z$, and therefore $$Z=PiPZPiP=PiZiP.$$ Thus restriction determines $Z$ uniquely. Conversely, for a relation $A=iAi$ supported on $F$, the relation $PAP$ belongs to $Q\mathcal R_HQ$, since $PAP=QAQ$, and $$i(PAP)i=(iPi)A(iPi)=A.$$ This proves bijectivity with the claimed inverse. For supported relations $A,B\in\mathcal R_F$, $$(PAP)(PBP)=PAPBP
           =PA(iPi)BP
           =PABP.$$ Hence the inverse, and therefore restriction, preserves multiplication. Finally $\rho(Q)=i$, the identity of $\mathcal R_F$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Amplifying a single added pair

Set <a id="amp:D"></a>$$t=64,\qquad
 D(h)=2^{\lfloor(h-2)/(2t-1)\rfloor}\quad(h\geq2).
 \label{amp:D}$$ The choice $t=64$ balances the two estimates proved below: $t^2$ pair additions each cost at least half the smaller-instance bound, while their total cost is at most $16t$ times the original loss. The resulting gain is $t/32=2$, at a reduction of $2t-1=127$ points. We prove a missing-set bound strong enough to survive passage to any of the corners above. The additional unit-lifting hypothesis permits conjugation by arbitrary permutations of $H$; Lemma <a href="#amp:minimal-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:minimal-corner">11</a> will supply it when we return to Theorem <a href="#amp:image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:image">[amp:image]</a>.

<a id="stmt-amp-single-edge"></a>
<div id="amp:single-edge" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13** (Cost of one added pair). *Let $m\geq1$, let $e\in\mathcal T_m$ be idempotent, and let $S\subseteq e\mathcal T_me$ be a submonoid with identity $e$. Let $H$ be a finite set of cardinality $h\geq2$. Suppose that $\phi:S\to\mathcal R_H$ is a unital surjective homomorphism such that $$z\sqsubseteq w\ \Longrightarrow\ \phi(w)\subseteq\phi(z)
 \qquad(z,w\in S),$$ and suppose that each permutation of $H$ has a lift that is a unit of $S$. If $a\in S$ is idempotent and, for distinct $x,y\in H$, $$\phi(a)=I_H\cup\{(x,y)\},$$ then $|\mathcal M_e(a)|\geq D(h)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-amp-single-edge"><summary>Proof of stmt-amp-single-edge</summary>

*Proof.* We induct on $h$, simultaneously over all the data in the statement, including $m$, $e$, $S$, and $\phi$. For $2\leq h\leq2t$, we have $D(h)=1$. If $\mathcal M_e(a)$ were empty, Lemma <a href="#diag:rectangles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:rectangles">6</a> would give $e\sqsubseteq a$. Order reversal would then give $I_H\cup\{(x,y)\}\subseteq I_H$, a contradiction.

Assume henceforth that $h\geq2t+1$, and put $c=|\mathcal M_e(a)|$. We will construct $t^2$ nested steps with total missing-set size at most $16tc$. Each step will contain a smaller instance of the Proposition, on $h-2t+1$ points.

**Step 1: A common budget for all pair additions.** Choose disjoint sets $$U=\{u_1,\ldots,u_t\},\qquad
 V=\{v_1,\ldots,v_t\},\qquad \{z\}$$ in $H$. Conjugating $a$ by unit lifts of permutations gives elements $s_i,s'_j\in S$ with <a id="amp:hub"></a>$$\phi(s_i)=I_H\cup\{(u_i,z)\},\qquad
 \phi(s'_j)=I_H\cup\{(z,v_j)\}.
 \label{amp:hub}$$ Indeed, permutation conjugation acts transitively on ordered pairs of distinct points, and the inverse of a unit lift maps to the inverse permutation. Explicitly, a unit $p\in S$ gives the conjugate $pap^{-1}$, with $pep^{-1}=e$. Applying Lemma <a href="#diag:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:transport">7</a> with $J=\mathcal M_e(a)$ therefore gives $$|\mathcal M_e(s_i)|\leq2c,\qquad
 |\mathcal M_e(s'_j)|\leq2c.$$ Consequently the single set $$J=\bigcup_{i=1}^t\mathcal M_e(s_i)
       \ \cup\ \bigcup_{j=1}^t\mathcal M_e(s'_j)
       \ \subseteq\mathcal C_e$$ has $|J|\leq4tc$, and the product inequality of Lemma <a href="#diag:missing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:missing">5</a> gives $\mathcal M_e(s_i s'_j)\subseteq J$ for every $i,j$.

Let $P_0=I_{H\setminus\{z\}}$. Choose an idempotent lift $b_0\in S$ of $P_0$, by taking an idempotent positive power of any lift, and define $$g_{ij}=b_0s_i s'_j b_0\in b_0Sb_0.$$ The image of $s_i s'_j$ consists of the identity and the three pairs $(u_i,z)$, $(z,v_j)$, and $(u_i,v_j)$. The outer factors $P_0$ remove the first two, so <a id="amp:pair-image"></a>$$\phi(g_{ij})=P_0\cup\{(u_i,v_j)\}.
 \label{amp:pair-image}$$ Figure <a href="#fig:hub" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:hub">3</a> illustrates this conversion of two generator families into a family indexed by $U\times V$.

<figure id="fig:hub">

<figcaption>The relation images of the hub construction, with identity pairs omitted. Composing the $u_{i} → z$ and $z → v_{j}$ additions creates $u_{i} → v_{j}$; sandwiching by $P_{0}$ removes the two pairs incident to $z$. The $2t$ generators yield all $t^{2}$ pair additions.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-hub"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:hub`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,font=\small,thick]
 \node (u) at (0,0) {$u_i$};
 \node (z) at (1.8,0) {$z$};
 \node (v) at (3.6,0) {$v_j$};
 \draw[->] (u)--node[above] {$\phi(s_i)$}(z);
 \draw[->] (z)--node[above] {$\phi(s'_j)$}(v);
 \node[align=center] at (1.8,-0.8) {Compose the two relations};
 \draw[->,double] (4.35,0)--node[above] {$P_0\,(\cdot)\,P_0$}(6.35,0);
 \node (uu) at (7.1,0) {$u_i$};
 \node (vv) at (9.5,0) {$v_j$};
 \draw[->] (uu)--node[above] {$\phi(g_{ij})$}(vv);
 \node[align=center] at (8.3,-0.8) {Remove endpoints at $z$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 13 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Diagram of relation composition and endpoint removal. On the left: three nodes $u_i$, $z$, and $v_j$ arranged horizontally. An arrow points from $u_i$ to $z$ labeled $\phi(s_i)$, and an arrow points from $z$ to $v_j$ labeled $\phi(s'_j)$; below them is the text "Compose the two relations". In the middle: a horizontal arrow pointing right labeled above by $P_0 (\cdot) P_0$. On the right: two nodes $u_i$ and $v_j$ with a single direct arrow from $u_i$ to $v_j$ labeled $\phi(g_{ij})$; below them is the text "Remove endpoints at $z$".
```


<a id="amp:common-budget"></a>
Since $b_0eb_0=b_0$, the common-set conclusion of Lemma <a href="#diag:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:transport">7</a> gives a *single* set $J_0\subseteq\mathcal C_{b_0}$ such that $$|J_0|\leq8tc,\qquad
 \mathcal M_{b_0}(g_{ij})\subseteq J_0
 \quad(1\leq i,j\leq t).
 \label{amp:common-budget}$$

<a id="amp:union"></a>
**Step 2: A nested chain spending that budget.** List the $t^2$ pairs of $U\times V$ in any order, without repetitions. Let $E_\ell$ be the set of the first $\ell$ pairs, and put $P_\ell=P_0\cup E_\ell$ for $0\leq\ell\leq t^2$. For any $E,E'\subseteq U\times V$, $$(P_0\cup E)(P_0\cup E')=P_0\cup E\cup E'.
 \label{amp:union}$$ To check this, $P_0$ acts as the identity on all the endpoints in question, and no pair of $E$ can be followed by a pair of $E'$, because $U\cap V=\varnothing$. In particular, every $P_\ell$ is idempotent.

<a id="amp:total-cost"></a>
If the pair at position $\ell$ is $(u_i,v_j)$, choose $b_\ell$ to be an idempotent positive power of $b_{\ell-1}g_{ij}b_{\ell-1}$. Inductively, Equation <a href="#amp:union" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="amp:union">[amp:union]</a> gives $\phi(b_\ell)=P_\ell$. The sandwich, and hence all its positive powers, is absorbed on both sides by $b_{\ell-1}$. Thus $$b_\ell\trianglelefteq b_{\ell-1}
 \qquad(1\leq\ell\leq t^2).$$ All these elements lie in the $b_0$-corner. Starting with $\mathcal M_{b_0}(b_0)=\varnothing$, repeated use of Lemma <a href="#diag:missing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:missing">5</a> and Equation <a href="#amp:common-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="amp:common-budget">[amp:common-budget]</a> shows that $$\mathcal M_{b_0}(b_\ell)\subseteq J_0
 \qquad(0\leq\ell\leq t^2).$$ Here taking a positive power cannot enlarge the missing set: all factors have the same missing set, and their union is unchanged. Proposition <a href="#nest:chain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nest:chain">10</a> now yields $$\sum_{\ell=1}^{t^2}\delta_\ell\leq16tc,
 \qquad
 \delta_\ell=|\mathcal M_{b_{\ell-1}}(b_\ell)|.
 \label{amp:total-cost}$$ We have bounded the total cost of the chain using only the $2t$ original conjugates. It remains to give an inductive lower bound for each of its $t^2$ steps.

<a id="amp:compressed-pair"></a>
**Step 3: A smaller instance inside every step.** Fix $\ell$, let $(u_i,v_j)$ be its newly added pair, and write $b=b_{\ell-1}$ and $P=P_{\ell-1}$. Retain the two endpoints of this pair and all points outside the construction: $$F=\{u_i,v_j\}\cup
       \bigl(H\setminus(U\cup V\cup\{z\})\bigr),
 \qquad i_F=I_F,\qquad Q=Pi_FP.$$ Then $|F|=h-2t+1\geq2$. Among the possible extra pairs of $P$, only $(u_i,v_j)$ has both endpoints in $F$, and this pair has not yet been added. Therefore $i_FPi_F=i_F$. By Lemma <a href="#amp:relation-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:relation-corner">12</a>, $Q\trianglelefteq P$ and restriction gives $Q\mathcal R_HQ\simeq\mathcal R_F$. Moreover, Equation <a href="#amp:union" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="amp:union">[amp:union]</a> gives $PP_\ell P=P_\ell$. Using $i_FQ=i_FP$ and $Qi_F=Pi_F$, we obtain $$i_F(QP_\ell Q)i_F
 =i_FP_\ell i_F
 =I_F\cup\{(u_i,v_j)\}.
 \label{amp:compressed-pair}$$

Consider the finite monoid $A=bSb$. The restriction of $\phi$ maps it onto $P\mathcal R_HP$: sandwiching any preimage by $b$ gives a preimage of its $P$-sandwich. Apply Lemma <a href="#amp:minimal-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:minimal-corner">11</a> *inside $A$* to the idempotent $Q$. We obtain an idempotent $d\in A$ with $\phi(d)=Q$, minimal there among idempotent lifts of $Q$. Its corner $dAd$ maps unitally onto $$Q(P\mathcal R_HP)Q=Q\mathcal R_HQ,$$ and all units of this image have unit lifts. Composing with restriction gives a unital surjective homomorphism <a id="amp:smaller-map"></a>$$\psi:dAd\longrightarrow\mathcal R_F,
 \qquad \psi(w)=i_F\phi(w)i_F.
 \label{amp:smaller-map}$$ This map is order-reversing because $\phi$ is and restriction preserves inclusion. It has unit lifts of every permutation of $F$, by the isomorphism in Lemma <a href="#amp:relation-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:relation-corner">12</a>. Also $dAd$ is a submonoid of $d\mathcal T_md$ with identity $d$. These observations verify all the map and domain hypotheses needed for induction.

Because $b_\ell\trianglelefteq b$, we have $b_\ell\in A$. By Equation <a href="#amp:compressed-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="amp:compressed-pair">[amp:compressed-pair]</a>, the element $db_\ell d\in dAd$ maps under $\psi$ to $I_F\cup\{(u_i,v_j)\}$. This relation is idempotent, so an idempotent positive power $v$ of $db_\ell d$ has the same image. Since $d\in A$ is idempotent, $dbd=d$. Apply Lemma <a href="#diag:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:transport">7</a> from the identity $b$ to the identity $d$, with context $(d,d)$ and missing set $\mathcal M_b(b_\ell)$. Then apply the power consequence of Lemma <a href="#diag:missing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="diag:missing">5</a> in the $d$-corner. This gives <a id="amp:step-cost"></a>$$|\mathcal M_d(v)|
 \leq |\mathcal M_d(db_\ell d)|
 \leq 2\delta_\ell.
 \label{amp:step-cost}$$ The induction hypothesis applies to $d$, $dAd$, $\psi$, and $v$, with the smaller parameter $|F|=h-2t+1$. It follows that $$2\delta_\ell\geq D(h-2t+1).$$

Summing this lower bound and using Equation <a href="#amp:total-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="amp:total-cost">[amp:total-cost]</a>, we conclude that $$16tc\ \geq\ \frac{t^2}{2}D(h-2t+1),
 \qquad
 c\ \geq\ \frac{t}{32}D(h-2t+1).$$ For $t=64$, the last expression is $2D(h-127)=D(h)$, by the definition of $D$. This completes the induction. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-amp-single-edge-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-amp-single-edge</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#amp:image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:image">[amp:image]</a>.* Apply Lemma <a href="#amp:minimal-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:minimal-corner">11</a> to $\phi_0$ and the idempotent $I_H$. It supplies an idempotent $e\in S_0$ such that $S=eS_0e$ maps unitally onto $\mathcal R_H$ and every permutation has a unit lift. The restricted map retains order reversal, and $S$ is a submonoid of $e\mathcal T_me$ with identity $e$.

Choose distinct $x,y\in H$ and any lift in $S$ of $I_H\cup\{(x,y)\}$. An idempotent positive power $a$ of that lift has the same image. Proposition <a href="#amp:single-edge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:single-edge">13</a> therefore gives $$D(h)\leq|\mathcal M_e(a)|\leq|\mathcal C_e|\leq2m,$$ as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="auto:section"></a>
### Finite computations and the main lower bound

We complete the concrete obligations deferred in Section <a href="#sec:diagrams" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:diagrams">2</a>: constructing the small source automaton and proving the finite-computation representation. The order-reversing map has already been derived in Proposition <a href="#auto:order-reversal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:order-reversal">4</a>; we then combine it with Theorem <a href="#amp:image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:image">[amp:image]</a> to finish the main proof. Throughout, we use the automaton model fixed in the introduction, including stay moves, finite acceptance, and acceptance in the initial configuration.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The source automaton

Fix a set $H$ of size $h\geq2$. Recall that the alphabet is $\Sigma_H=\mathcal R_H$, the word product is $r(w)$ with $r(\varepsilon)=I_H$, and <a id="auto:language"></a>$$\label{auto:language}
 L_H=\{w\in\Sigma_H^*:r(w)\ne\varnothing\}.$$

<a id="stmt-auto-source"></a>
<div id="auto:source" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *The language $L_H$ is recognized by a 2NFA with exactly $h+2$ states.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-source"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-source</summary>

*Proof.* Use one initial state $q_{\mathrm{in}}$, the $h$ states in $H$, and one accepting state $q_{\mathrm{acc}}$, all distinct. From $q_{\mathrm{in}}$ on the left endmarker, the machine moves right into any chosen state of $H$. On a letter $R\in\mathcal R_H$, it may move right from $p\in H$ to $q\in H$ precisely when $(p,q)\in R$. At the right endmarker, every state in $H$ has a stay transition to $q_{\mathrm{acc}}$. There are no other transitions, and $q_{\mathrm{acc}}$ is the only accepting state.

On $R_1\cdots R_k$, an accepting computation is exactly a sequence $p_0,\ldots,p_k\in H$ with $(p_{i-1},p_i)\in R_i$ for $1\leq i\leq k$. Such a sequence exists exactly when the relation product is nonempty. For $k=0$, the initial move reaches the right endmarker directly, and the machine accepts. This agrees with $r(\varepsilon)=I_H\ne\varnothing$. ◻

</details>

<a id="auto:finite-computations"></a>
#### Finite computations as diagram paths

We now prove the representation promised in Lemma <a href="#auto:representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:representation">3</a>. The construction records arbitrary finite computations, including stay moves and repeated crossings of the same cut. It does not require termination of every computation.

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-source-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-source</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#auto:representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:representation">3</a>.* Let $B$ be the $s$-state automaton in the lemma, with state set $Q$ and initial state $q_0$. Introduce one new state $q_\dagger$. From every original accepting state, at every cell, add a stay transition to $q_\dagger$. In state $q_\dagger$, move right regardless of the scanned symbol, finally exiting past the right endmarker in state $q_\dagger$. This last exit is only a device for representing acceptance, not a transition of the original machine. All original transitions remain subject to the endmarker restrictions.

A finite augmented computation from the original initial configuration to the designated exit exists if and only if $B$ accepts. Indeed, an accepting original computation can be followed by the added stay and rightward sweep. Conversely, the first entry into $q_\dagger$ must come from an original accepting configuration. This argument includes the case that $q_0$ itself is accepting. Infinite computations create no additional finite successful computation.

Put $\widehat Q=Q\cup\{q_\dagger\}$ and $m=s+1$. Take the directional label sets to be two disjoint copies $$D^+=\{q^+:q\in\widehat Q\},
  \qquad
  D^-=\{q^-:q\in\widehat Q\}.$$ For a cell symbol $c$, including either endmarker with its boundary rules, let $E_c$ be the relation on $\widehat Q$ given by the augmented stay transitions. Let $T_c^+$ and $T_c^-$ be the relations given by the augmented rightward and leftward transitions, respectively; their second coordinates record the state *after* the move. At the right endmarker, $T_c^+$ includes only the designated augmented exit, and at the left endmarker $T_c^-$ is empty. Define a diagram $d_c$ by $$\begin{aligned}
 (q^+,p^+)\in F_{d_c}
 &\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 (q^-,p^+)\in R_{d_c}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 (q,p)\in E_c^*T_c^+,\\
 (q^-,p^-)\in B_{d_c}
 &\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 (q^+,p^-)\in L_{d_c}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 (q,p)\in E_c^*T_c^-.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus each local diagram edge represents a finite sequence of stays, possibly empty, followed by one move out of the cell. The incoming sign specifies the side of entry, not additional machine memory; the transition rules themselves depend only on the state and symbol.

Set $\lambda=d_{\mathrm{left}}$ and $\rho=d_{\mathrm{right}}$ for the two endmarkers, and set $$\tau(c_1\cdots c_k)=d_{c_1}\cdots d_{c_k},
  \qquad \tau(\varepsilon)=1_{\mathcal T_m}.$$ This is a monoid homomorphism. Every finite augmented computation ending in the designated exit decomposes into successive visits to individual cells, each consisting of finitely many stays and one exit move. These visits give a path through the product of the cell diagrams. In the opposite direction, each local edge in a finite diagram path has a finite transition witness. Expanding these witnesses and concatenating them gives an augmented computation, because the exit state from one cell is exactly the entry state to the next. Neither direction assumes that a cut is crossed at most once.

The fictitious entry into the left endmarker with label $q_0^+$ represents the initial configuration; it is not an additional machine transition. No path can leave the tape to the left, and a path can leave to the right only through the designated exit. Hence <a href="#auto:recognition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="auto:recognition">[auto:recognition]</a> holds with $a=q_0^+$ and $b=q_\dagger^+$. For the empty word, the two endmarker cells are adjacent, and the identity $\tau(\varepsilon)$ gives precisely their product. A stay cycle causes no difficulty: only its finite traversals are represented in $E_c^*$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-conventions"></a>
<div id="auto:conventions" class="remark">

*Remark 15* (Other standard conventions). The exponential conclusion also survives the usual changes of starting position or acceptance convention, with possible changes to the additive state offsets. To use our representation for a machine whose convention requires a positive transition into an accepting state, add a fresh nonaccepting initial copy with the same outgoing transitions, keeping all original states and transition destinations. This prevents the initial configuration alone from accepting, while preserving every acceptance after a transition. Starting on the first input cell can be simulated from the left endmarker by one additional initial state. If success must occur at a designated marker or on a specified exit, enable the added success routine only at that local accepting event. These versions of the path representation use $m=s+O(1)$ labels per sign, and the source automaton still uses $h+O(1)$ states. The exact offsets in Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a> refer to the convention fixed in the introduction.

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-conventions"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-conventions</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a>.* For any $n\geq4$, let $h=n-2$, choose a set $H$ of size $h$, and use the $n$-state source automaton from Lemma <a href="#auto:source" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:source">14</a>. If an $s$-state 2NFA recognizes its complement, then Proposition <a href="#auto:order-reversal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:order-reversal">4</a> supplies the order-reversing relation image required by Theorem <a href="#amp:image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="amp:image">[amp:image]</a>. With $m=s+1$, that bound gives $$2(s+1)\geq 2^{\lfloor(h-2)/127\rfloor}
            =2^{\lfloor(n-4)/127\rfloor}.$$ Rearranging proves the stated lower bound. Since its right-hand side grows exponentially with $n$, no fixed polynomial can bound the complementation cost for all finite alphabets. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The determinization consequence

The lower bound also applies indirectly to deterministic simulation. Here we use one external automata transformation: deterministic two-way automata can be complemented with linear state overhead independent of the alphabet, even when the original computation may fail to halt (Geffert, Mereghetti, and Pighizzini 2007) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[GeffertMereghettiPighizzini2007](#ref-GeffertMereghettiPighizzini2007)]. The possible nonaccepting loops are the reason that exchanging accepting and rejecting states alone does not give this transformation.

<a id="stmt-auto-determinization"></a>
<div id="auto:determinization" class="corollary">

**Corollary 16**. *There are absolute constants $c>0$ and $n_0$ such that, for every $n\ge n_0$, every 2DFA recognizing the language of the $n$-state 2NFA $A_n$ in Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a> has at least $$c\,2^{\lfloor(n-4)/127\rfloor}$$ states. Hence no polynomial state bound independent of the finite alphabet can determinize all 2NFAs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-determinization"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-determinization</summary>

*Proof.* We use only the linear-overhead conclusion of (Geffert, Mereghetti, and Pighizzini 2007) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GeffertMereghettiPighizzini2007](#ref-GeffertMereghettiPighizzini2007)], allowing constant-factor changes for model conventions. The standard marker model and its normalization are described in the precursor (Geffert, Mereghetti, and Pighizzini 2005, sec. 2 and Lemma 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GeffertMereghettiPighizzini2005](#ref-GeffertMereghettiPighizzini2005)]. Stay moves can be replaced by a two-step excursion that stores the destination state and the return direction in a fixed number of copies of the state set. The excursion goes left at the right endmarker and right elsewhere. Even on the empty word, the two distinct endmarker cells provide the needed adjacent cell. Auxiliary states are nonaccepting, and the destination state is entered only on returning to the original cell. A reached accepting configuration, including the initial configuration, can instead start a fixed marker sweep to a designated accepting halt. Missing transitions can halt and reject. These changes require $O(s+1)$ states for an $s$-state 2DFA and preserve finite acceptance. The cited transformation supplies the substantive additional step of handling infinite nonaccepting computations.

Thus an $s$-state deterministic recognizer for $L(A_n)$ has a deterministic complement, also a 2NFA, with at most $C(s+1)$ states for an absolute constant $C$. Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a> gives $$C(s+1)\ge \tfrac12\,2^{\lfloor(n-4)/127\rfloor}-1.$$ For all sufficiently large $n$ this implies the asserted bound with an absolute $c>0$. Since the same alphabets are finite for every $n$, it also excludes every polynomial bound uniform over alphabets. ◻

</details>

For comparison, the companion theorem (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[CompanionDeterminization2026](#ref-CompanionDeterminization2026)] gives $$4(s+2)^2\geq 2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}$$ for an $s$-state deterministic recognizer of the same language $L_H$. That theorem also states the improvement from $s+2$ to $s+1$ when the initial configuration counts as acceptance, as it does here. Its exponent therefore grows as $h/62$, compared with $h/127$ in Corollary <a href="#auto:determinization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="auto:determinization">16</a>. The source constructions use $h+3$ and $h+2$ states, respectively: the companion includes a rejecting sink, which the partial source automaton above does not need. Its matching-diagram proof gives a stronger deterministic bound independently of the complementation theorem proved here.

      "source_evidence": [

          "anchor": "fig:diagram",
          "exact_quote": "The four relations of a path diagram. Each arrow represents a\nrelation between entire label sets, not a single deterministic edge.\nIn a product, an exit enters the neighboring segment with the same label.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,thick,font=\\small]\n \\fill[black!3] (0.4,-0.35) rectangle (6.6,2.35);\n \\draw[dashed,black!45] (0.4,-0.4)--(0.4,2.4);\n \\draw[dashed,black!45] (6.6,-0.4)--(6.6,2.4);\n \\node (lp) at (1,2) {$D^+$};\n \\node (lm) at (1,0) {$D^-$};\n \\node (rp) at (6,2) {$D^+$};\n \\node (rm) at (6,0) {$D^-$};\n \\draw[->] (lp)--node[above] {$F_a$}(rp);\n \\draw[->] (rm)--node[below] {$B_a$}(lm);\n \\draw[->] (lp) to[bend right=35] node[left] {$L_a$}(lm);\n \\draw[->] (rm) to[bend right=35] node[right] {$R_a$}(rp);\n \\node at (3.5,1) {segment $a$};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-0.15,2) {entry};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-0.15,0) {exit};\n \\node[anchor=west] at (7.15,2) {exit};\n \\node[anchor=west] at (7.15,0) {entry};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A path diagram for \"segment a\" showing four relations between boundary label sets. On the left boundary, there are label sets $D^+$ (with label \"entry\" to its left) and $D^-$ (with label \"exit\" to its left), connected by a downward curved arrow labeled $L_a$ from $D^+$ to $D^-$. On the right boundary, there are label sets $D^+$ (with label \"exit\" to its right) and $D^-$ (with label \"entry\" to its right), connected by an upward curved arrow labeled $R_a$ from $D^-$ to $D^+$. A horizontal arrow pointing from left $D^+$ to right $D^+$ is labeled $F_a$. A horizontal arrow pointing from right $D^-$ to left $D^-$ is labeled $B_a$. Centered between the arrows is the text \"segment a\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The weighted containment argument in the chain bound. All classes shown have one fixed sign and contain no protected label. Each new class contains either a whole nonmissing old class or two distinct whole old classes. A missing old class has weight $1/2$; every other old class has weight $1$. The displayed regions express set containment schematically. Distinct new classes cannot use the same whole old class, so their unit weight requirements add.",
      "figure_id": "fig:nesting-budget",
      "pdf_page": 10,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:nesting-budget",
          "exact_quote": "The weighted containment argument in the chain bound. All\nclasses shown have one fixed sign and contain no protected label.\nEach new class contains either a whole nonmissing old class or two\ndistinct whole old classes. A missing old class h",

      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\\small,thick]\n \\draw[rounded corners] (0,0) rectangle (5,2.3);\n \\draw[rounded corners] (6,0) rectangle (11,2.3);\n \\node at (2.5,1.95) {one new unprotected class};\n \\node at (8.5,1.95) {one new unprotected class};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners,fill=blue!7,align=center,\n       inner xsep=6pt,inner ysep=5pt] at (2.5,0.95)\n       {old nonmissing class\\\\weight $1$};\n \\node[draw,dashed,rounded corners,fill=black!4,align=center,\n       inner xsep=6pt,inner ysep=5pt] at (7.3,0.95)\n       {old class\\\\weight $\\ge\\frac12$};\n \\node[draw,dashed,rounded corners,fill=black!4,align=center,\n       inner xsep=6pt,inner ysep=5pt] at (9.7,0.95)\n       {old class\\\\weight $\\ge\\frac12$};\n \\node at (5.5,1.1) {or};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Schematic diagram showing two alternative cases separated by the label \"or\". On the left: a rounded rectangle labeled \"one new unprotected class\" enclosing an inner solid rectangle labeled \"old nonmissing class\" and \"weight 1\". On the right: a rounded rectangle labeled \"one new unprotected class\" enclosing two adjacent dashed rectangles side by side, each labeled \"old class\" and \"weight $\\ge \\frac{1}{2}$\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The relation images of the hub construction, with identity pairs omitted. Composing the $u_{i} → z$ and $z → v_{j}$ additions creates $u_{i} → v_{j}$; sandwiching by $P_{0}$ removes the two pairs incident to $z$. The $2t$ generators yield all $t^{2}$ pair additions.",
      "figure_id": "fig:hub",

          "exact_quote": "The relation images of the hub construction, with identity\npairs omitted. Composing the $u_i\\to z$ and $z\\to v_j$ additions\ncreates $u_i\\to v_j$; sandwiching by $P_0$ removes the two pairs\nincident to $z$. The $2t$ generators yield all $t^2",
